Ensemble Optimal Control for Managing Drug Resistance in Cancer Therapies
Authors: Alessandro Scagliotti, Federico Scagliotti, Laura Deborah Locati, Federico Sottotetti
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
In this paper, we explore the application of ensemble optimal control to derive enhanced strategies for pharmacological cancer treatment, and we tackle the problem of the long-term management of the disease, i.e., when the complete eradication of the tumor is not achievable. In particular, we focus on moving beyond the classical clinical approach of giving the patient the maximal tolerated drug dose (MTD), which does not properly exploit the fight among sensitive and resistant cells for the available resources. Here, we employ a Lotka-Volterra model to describe the competing subpopulations, and we enclose this system within the ensemble control framework. In the first part, we establish general results suitable for application to various cancers. Then, we carry out numerical simulations in the setting of prostate cancer treated with androgen deprivation therapy, yielding a computed policy that is reminiscent of the medical ‘active surveillance’ paradigm. Finally, inspired by the numerical evidence, we propose a variant of the celebrated adaptive therapy (AT), which we call ‘Off-On’ AT.

Introduction

In this paper, we consider a simple differential model for drug resistance in pharmacological cancer treatments, and we address the uncertainty that affects the dynamics and the Cauchy datum using tools of ensemble control of ODEs.

In this framework, we aim to find a common policy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \mapsto u(t)$$\end{document}t↦u(t) for simultaneously driving every system of the parametrized family (1.1). This is typically the case when the parameters that appear in the dynamics are subject to statistical errors, and we seek to compute a control that works in every situation (see Ruths and Li 2012). Problems involving ensembles are a timely source of interest for researchers working in Mathematical Control. Indeed, on the one hand, among the recent theoretical contributions in the field, we report (Aronna et al. 2025b; Bettiol and Khalil 2019, 2024) for averaged optimal control problems, and Abdel Wahab and Bettiol (2025); Scagliotti (2025) for the minimax optimization. Moreover, the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation related to ensemble control has been considered in Aronna et al. (2026). On the other hand, owing to its flexibility, this framework has found several applications, e.g. in quantum control (Augier et al. 2018; Chittaro and Gauthier 2018; Robin et al. 2022), and in the mathematical modeling of Deep Learning (see Ruiz-Balet and Zuazua 2023; Álvarez-López et al. 2024, 2025; Cipriani et al. 2025) and of Reinforcement Learning (see Murray and Palladino 2018; Pesare et al. 2021), to mention a few. To the best of our knowledge, here we employ for the first time the viewpoint of ensemble control for tuning drug dosage in cancer therapy. Nonetheless, the interplay between optimal control and tumor modeling has a long and glorious story (see the textbook Schättler and Ledzewicz 2015 for an introduction and for a historical overview). Among recent contributions, we refer the reader to Cunningham et al. (2018); Ledzewicz and Schättler (2024), and to Edduweh and Roy (2024) for a Liouville equation for prostate cancer. Moreover, we mention (Aguadé-Gorgorió et al. 2024) for a generalized Lotka-Volterra model for the ecological variety of tumors environment, and Morselli et al. (2023, 2026) for models on oncolytic viruses.

In the first part of the paper (Section 2), we develop in a general setting a framework for ensemble optimal control for cancer therapy. Model parameters and variables for the system in eq. (1.3).

In fact, we can consider even more refined models than the one in (1.3), where the drug concentration around the cancer cells does coincide with the drug dose. In a more realistic description, concentration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto C(t)$$\end{document}t↦C(t) and dose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto D(t)$$\end{document}t↦D(t) can be related via a differential equation, such as, e.g.,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{C}(t) = -k^C C(t) + k^I \frac{D(t)}{D_{\max }} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C˙(t)=-kCC(t)+kID(t)Dmax.In this case, we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z:=(C,X_1,\ldots ,X_n)$$\end{document}Z:=(C,X1,…,Xn) and we modify accordingly (1.3) so that the induced death rate and the inter-species evolution is triggered by C(t) in place of D(t). Moreover, a direct computation shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto Z(t)$$\end{document}t↦Z(t) solves as well a control-affine system of the form (1.2), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto D(t)$$\end{document}t↦D(t) is once again the control input. We insist on the fact that the affine dependence in the control variable is crucial for our analysis—in particular, for the results contained in Theorem 2.2. Once a suitable control-affine model has been selected, the ensemble optimal control approach consists in finding a drug administration policy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto D(t)$$\end{document}t↦D(t) that takes into account the uncertainty that affects (some of) the model parameters. The goal of this paper is to propose strategies that exhibit enhanced performances in the long-term disease management. In particular, we focus on moving beyond the classical clinical approach of giving to the patient the maximal tolerated dose (MTD), which consists in setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}_{\textrm{MTD}}(t) = {D_{\max }}$$\end{document}DMTD(t)=Dmax for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0 in eq. (1.3). When sensitive and resistant clones compete for the available resources—as in solid cancers1—, MTD has already been shown to be sub-optimal. Indeed, it leads to the extinction of the sensitive cells and to the emerging of a resistant population (Silva et al. 2012). Here, we aim to obtain a policy of drug dosage by solving an ensemble optimal control problem over a fixed time horizon [0, T], where T is pre-determined (see Remark 8 for the choice in our simulations). To do this, the key-steps are the definition of a compact set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta $$\end{document}Θ where the uncertain parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ vary, and the introduction of a probability measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in \mathcal {P}(\Theta )$$\end{document}μ∈P(Θ) that describes our knowledge about the distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ. For instance, one may consider repeated measurements of tumor burden in a cohort of patients over the course of therapy, where treatment interruptions (vacations) are employed. Model parameters for each patient could then be estimated via standard parameter identification procedures (see, e.g., Nelles 2020). The resulting collection of estimates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _1,\ldots ,\theta _k$$\end{document}θ1,…,θk may be interpreted as independent samples from the underlying distribution, yielding the definition of an empirical probability measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu :=\frac{1}{k} \sum _{j=1}^k \delta _{\theta _j}$$\end{document}μ:=1k∑j=1kδθj, which can be used to study new or existing treatment schedules through ensemble optimal control. Hence, in this framework, the dosage strategy is computed through the minimization of a functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}:\mathcal {U}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}J:U→R of the form1.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {J}({D}) :=\int _\Theta \int _0^T \ell \left( {N_D^\theta (t)} \right) \,\textrm{d}{t} \,\textrm{d}\mu (\theta ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J(D):=∫Θ∫0TℓNDθ(t)dtdμ(θ),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}:=\{{D} \in L^2([0,T],\mathbb {R}): 0\le {D(t)}\le {D_{\max }} \, \text{ for } \text{ a.e. } {t}\}$$\end{document}U:={D∈L2([0,T],R):0≤D(t)≤Dmaxfora.e.t}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${N_D^\theta (t)}$$\end{document}NDθ(t) is derived from eq. (1.3), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell :\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ℓ:R→R is a proper penalization cost on the tumor size. For the problem of minimizing (1.4), we provide a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-convergence result (see Theorem 2.2), and we provide the explicit expression for the projected gradient field (Proposition 2.5 and 2.8), moving beyond (Scagliotti 2023a, b), where the control variable was unconstrained. Finally, we observe that, roughly speaking, minimizing (1.4) means that we look for a control that ‘does on average a good job on the elements of the ensemble’. In this regard, we point out that, even though a minimax ensemble control formulation is a viable option (see Scagliotti 2025), in the application that we are considering here it is rather distant from the clinical practice. Indeed, the focus on the improvement of the least favorable cases usually comes at the expenses of a performance degradation on the most likely ones.

In the second part of the paper, we study a simplified system that describes the dynamics of the two subpopulations of a tumor (sensitive and resistant) when the patient is undergoing a pharmacological treatment. Following (Cunningham et al. 2018; Strobl et al. 2021), in the present analysis we assume that the resistant subpopulation consists of descendants of pre-existing clones, and we do not include a mechanism of acquired (secondary) resistance. Namely, adopting the same notations as in Strobl et al. (2021), we render the competition for the limited resources through a Lotka-Volterra system, and we address the following ODE in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2:1.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\begin{matrix} & {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \dot{S}(t) = r_S\left( 1 - \frac{S(t)+R(t)}{K} \right) \left( 1 - 2d_D\frac{D(t)}{D_{\max }} \right) S(t) - d_T S(t), \\ \dot{R}(t) = r_R\left( 1 - \frac{S(t)+R(t)}{K} \right) R(t) - d_T R(t), \end{array}\right. } \\ & \quad N(t) :=S(t) + R(t), \end{matrix}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S˙(t)=rS1-S(t)+R(t)K1-2dDD(t)DmaxS(t)-dTS(t),R˙(t)=rR1-S(t)+R(t)KR(t)-dTR(t),N(t):=S(t)+R(t),where the time-dependent functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto S(t)$$\end{document}t↦S(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto R(t)$$\end{document}t↦R(t) denote, respectively, the amount of cells of the tumor population at the instant t that are sensitive and resistant to a certain drug, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto N(t)$$\end{document}t↦N(t) accounts for the total population (see also Table 2). In eq. (1.5) the control is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto D(t)$$\end{document}t↦D(t), which takes value in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,D_{\max }]$$\end{document}[0,Dmax] and represents the drug concentration in the tumor environment. In our model, we assume that the fraction of cells killed by the drug is linearly proportional to the given dose. This classical hypothesis, formulated in Norton and Simon (1977), has been widely adopted in the literature (see, e.g., Schättler and Ledzewicz 2015; Strobl et al. 2021).Table 2Model parameters and variables for the simplified two-population model in eq. Model parameters and variables for the simplified two-population model in eq. (1.5).

In Section 3 we take advantage of the parameters estimates reported in Strobl et al. (2021) and we formulate an ensemble optimal control problem for prostate cancer (non-metastatic and castration sensitive, m0CSPC) treated with androgen deprivation therapy (ADT). We compare the performances using the ‘time to progression’ of the disease (TTP), and we adopt the Adaptive Therapy (AT) proposed in Gatenby et al. (2009); Zhang et al. (2017) and MTD as benchmark strategies. When the integral cost \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell $$\end{document}ℓ is linear, the resulting optimal strategy turns out to behave closely to the MTD protocol. However, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell $$\end{document}ℓ has a hyperbolic profile, the computed policy suggests a delayed starting of the therapy, and on average it outperforms MTD and AT in terms of ‘time to progression’ (see Tables 4 and 6). Moreover, this strategy is reminiscent of medical paradigm known as ‘active surveillance’ (Karim et al. 2025; Preisser et al. 2024). While this feature may be of conceptual interest, a significant limitation of the computed policy is that, during the initial phase, it permits substantial tumor growth, which may limit its practical applicability. Finally, to amend this point, we propose a variant of the AT that we call ‘Off-On’ Adaptive Therapy, which combines the ‘active surveillance’ paradigm (i.e., delayed starting of the therapy) with adaptive periods of treatment vacation (Gatenby et al. 2009; Zhang et al. 2017), showing promising results (see Table 7).

Ensemble Optimal Control Formulation: Theoretical Framework

In this section, we perform a general analysis for the ensemble control problems formulated in eq. (1.3). First, we show how to enclose eq. (1.3) within the theoretical framework of ensembles of control-affine systems tackled in Scagliotti (2023b, 2025). Then, we define the ensemble optimal control problem, and we show that it admits minimizers and that a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-convergence result holds. Finally, we study the gradient of the functional involved in the optimization problem.

Definition of the Dynamics

We recall that an ensemble of control-affine systems has the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{X}^\theta = \tilde{F}_0^\theta (X^\theta ) + \tilde{F}_1^\theta (X^\theta )u$$\end{document}X˙θ=F~0θ(Xθ)+F~1θ(Xθ)u. Lemma 2.1

Let us consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}\in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}D∈U defined as in eq. For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta $$\end{document}θ∈Θ, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${X_D^\theta (0)}\in \Delta ^{\theta }$$\end{document}XDθ(0)∈Δθ, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${X_D^\theta (t)} \in \Delta ^{\theta }$$\end{document}XDθ(t)∈Δθ (and, in particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_D^\theta (t) \in \Delta $$\end{document}XDθ(t)∈Δ) for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in [0,T]$$\end{document}t∈[0,T].

Proof

See Appendix A. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Unfortunately, the vector fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{F}_0^\theta ,\tilde{F}_1^\theta :\mathbb {R}^{n} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{n}$$\end{document}F~0θ,F~1θ:Rn→Rn involved in the differential model (2.1) do not satisfy the usual working assumptions, which require the fields to be globally Lipschitz continuous and to have a sub-linear growth (see, e.g., Scagliotti 2025, Hypothesis 2.1). Ensemble Optimal Control Problem and Related Functional

We shall propose a therapy schedule resulting in the (approximate) solution of an ensemble optimal control problem. To this end, we assume that we are given a probability measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in \mathcal {P}(\Theta )$$\end{document}μ∈P(Θ) on the space of the unknown parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ of the control system in eq. (2.1) and (2.2). Throughout the paper, the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta \subset \mathbb {R}^{m}$$\end{document}Θ⊂Rm is assumed to be compact. Recalling the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U in eq. (2.6), we consider the ensemble optimal control problem associated to the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}:\mathcal {U}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}J:U→R defined as2.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {J}({D}) :=\int _\Theta \int _0^T \ell \left( {N_D}^\theta ({t} )\right) \,\textrm{d}{t} \,\textrm{d}\mu (\theta ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J(D):=∫Θ∫0TℓNDθ(t)dtdμ(θ),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_D^\theta (t)=\sum _{i=1}^nX_{D\ i}^\theta (t)$$\end{document}NDθ(t)=∑i=1nXDiθ(t), with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{D\, 1}^\theta , \ldots , X_{D\, n}^\theta $$\end{document}XD1θ,…,XDnθ solutions of eq. (3.3) corresponding to the admissible control \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}\in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}D∈U and to the parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta $$\end{document}θ∈Θ, and where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell :\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ℓ:R→R is a cost function of class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2.

Remark 1

The function that we integrate in eq. (2.8) depends on the total cancer population \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${N_D^\theta (t)}$$\end{document}NDθ(t) at every instant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T]$$\end{document}t∈[0,T]. This choice has a precise interpretation in the model. Indeed, in a real-world scenario, it is not realistic to have access separately to the sizes of the n different subpopulations.

Remark 2

The functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J introduced in eq. (2.8) designs an averaged ensemble optimal control problem, as we saturate the dependence on the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ by averaging with respect to the probability measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ. An alternative paradigm consists in optimizing with respect to the worst-case scenario. Namely, this would result in addressing the minimax problem induced by the functional2.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {J}_{\max } :=\max _{\theta \in \Theta } \left( \int _0^T \ell \left( {N_D}^\theta ({t} )\right) \,\textrm{d}{t} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Jmax:=maxθ∈Θ∫0TℓNDθ(t)dt,recently studied in Scagliotti (2025). However, in the medical application that we are considering in the present paper, a minimax formulation can lead to over-conservative strategies. Indeed, it is more natural—and closer to the clinical practice—to design treatment strategies that are effective for the majority of the patients, rather than trying to achieve the best possible outcome on the worst-cases. This is due to the fact that the improvement on the least favorable systems of the ensemble usually comes at the expenses of a performance degradation on the most likely ones (see Scagliotti 2025, Section 6). Nevertheless, for comparison, in Section 3 we include numerical simulations addressing the minimax ensemble control problem.

Below, we show that the ensemble optimal control problem consisting in minimizing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J admits solution. Moreover, using the tools of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-convergence, we establish a ‘stability result’ for the minimizers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J with respect to perturbations in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ. Given a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mu _{k})_{{k}\ge 1}\in \mathcal {P}(\Theta )$$\end{document}(μk)k≥1∈P(Θ), we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{k}\rightharpoonup ^* \mu $$\end{document}μk⇀∗μ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${k}\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞ to denote the weak-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$*$$\end{document}∗ convergence of probability measures, i.e.,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{{k}\rightarrow \infty } \int _\Theta \phi (\theta )\,\textrm{d}\mu _{k}(\theta ) = \int _\Theta \phi (\theta )\,\textrm{d}\mu (\theta ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limk→∞∫Θϕ(θ)dμk(θ)=∫Θϕ(θ)dμ(θ)for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi :\Theta \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ϕ:Θ→R bounded and continuous.

Theorem 2.2

The functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}:\mathcal {U}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}J:U→R defined in eq. (2.8) admits minimizer in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U.

Moreover, given a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mu _{k})_{{k}\ge 1}\in \mathcal {P}(\Theta )$$\end{document}(μk)k≥1∈P(Θ) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{k}\rightharpoonup ^* \mu $$\end{document}μk⇀∗μ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${k}\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞, if we define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {J}_{k}({D}) :=\int _\Theta \int _0^T \ell \left( { N_D^\theta (t )}\right) \,\textrm{d}t \,\textrm{d}\mu _{k}(\theta ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Jk(D):=∫Θ∫0TℓNDθ(t)dtdμk(θ)for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} }\mathcal {J}_{k}({D}^\star _{k})$$\end{document}minUJ=limk→∞Jk(Dk⋆), and every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-weak limiting point of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({D}^\star _{k})_{{k}\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Dk⋆)k≥1 is a minimizer for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J.

Proof

The proof of the first part follows using the direct method of the Calculus of Variations, considering the weak topology of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2. More precisely, the lower semi-continuity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J is contained as a particular case of the proof of (Scagliotti 2023b, Theorem 3.2). For the weak coercivity, we recall that the space of admissible controls \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U is strongly closed and convex, hence it is weakly closed. Moreover, it is contained in the ball of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 centered at the origin and with radius \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{T}$$\end{document}T. Therefore, the whole domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J is weakly compact, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J admits minimizer. The second part part of the statement descends from the fact that the sequence of functionals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {J}_{k})_{{k}\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Jk)k≥1 is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-convergent to the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J with respect to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-weak topology (see Scagliotti 2023b, Theorem 4.6). Hence, observing that the functionals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {J}_{k})_{{k}\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Jk)k≥1 are defined on the same weakly compact domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U, the thesis follows by using (Scagliotti 2023b, Corollary 4.8). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 3

The second part of Theorem 2.2 plays a pivotal role in the applications. Indeed, each evaluation of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J requires the computation of the trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto {X_D}^\theta (t)$$\end{document}t↦XDθ(t) solving eq. (2.1) and (2.2) for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \textrm{supp}(\mu )$$\end{document}θ∈supp(μ). When the support of the measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in \mathcal {P}(\Theta )$$\end{document}μ∈P(Θ) contains infinitely many elements, this turns out to be completely impractical. In the case we approximate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ with a discrete measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{k}$$\end{document}μk with finite support, Theorem 2.2 ensures that any minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}_{k}$$\end{document}Jk is a good competitor as well for the original objective functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J. Finally, we observe that the construction of discrete approximating measures is an active research field (see, e.g., Mérigot et al. 2021; Auricchio et al. 2024; Auricchio and Zhang 2024; Auricchio et al. 2024)

Remark 4

We recall that the Pontryagin Maximum Principle (PMP) provides necessary conditions for optimal controls (see, e.g., Bressan and Piccoli 2007, Section 6 for a general introduction). In the framework of averaged ensemble optimal control, the PMP has been studied in Bettiol and Khalil (2019), and it provides insights on the structure of minimizers. More precisely, in our setting where the cost does not explicitly depend on the control input, from (Bettiol and Khalil 2019, Theorem 3.3) it follows that, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} For more details, see (Vinter 2005) and (Scagliotti 2025, Remark 5.10). In view of the analogy between eq. Gradient of the Objective Functional

In this part, we carry out the computations for the gradient of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J that we aim at minimizing. We report that the findings that we show below can be applied as well when we deal with an approximated functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}_{k}$$\end{document}Jk related to a measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{k} \approx \mu $$\end{document}μk≈μ. A main difficulty arises from the fact that admissible controls are constrained to the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,D_{\max }]$$\end{document}[0,Dmax], so that the control space is convex but not linear; to address this, we develop here a careful argument.

For this reason, it is convenient to introduce the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}':L^2([0,T], \mathbb {R})\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}J′:L2([0,T],R)→R defined according to eq. (2.8) for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D} \in L^2([0,T], \mathbb {R})$$\end{document}D∈L2([0,T],R). More precisely, we set2.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {J}'({D}):=\int _\Theta \int _0^T \ell \left( {N_D^\theta (t)}\right) \,\textrm{d}{t} \,\textrm{d}\mu (\theta ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J′(D):=∫Θ∫0TℓNDθ(t)dtdμ(θ),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_D^\theta (t)=\sum _{i=1}^nX_{D\ i}^\theta (t)$$\end{document}NDθ(t)=∑i=1nXDiθ(t), with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_D^\theta = \left( X_{D\ 1}^\theta , \ldots , X_{D\ n}^\theta \right) $$\end{document}XDθ=XD1θ,…,XDnθ solving2.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}{t}} {X_D^\theta }({t}) = F_0^\theta \left( {X_D^\theta } ({t}) \right) + F_1^\theta \left( {X_D^\theta } ({t}) \right) {D}({t}), \qquad {X_{D\ i}}^\theta (0) = {f_i N_0} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtXDθ(t)=F0θXDθ(t)+F1θXDθ(t)D(t),XDiθ(0)=fiN0for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta $$\end{document}θ∈Θ. In other words, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}'$$\end{document}J′ is the extension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J (which is defined only on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U) to the whole \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2, as illustrated in the next lemma. We observe that, in order to ensure that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}'$$\end{document}J′ is well-defined for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}\in L^2([0,T], \mathbb {R})$$\end{document}D∈L2([0,T],R), it is crucial to consider the truncated vector fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_0^\theta , F_1^\theta $$\end{document}F0θ,F1θ introduced in eq. Lemma 2.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}:\mathcal {U}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}J:U→R and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}':L^2([0,T],\mathbb {R})\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}J′:L2([0,T],R)→R be defined as in eq. (2.8) and eq. (2.12), respectively. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${X_D^\theta }(0) = \left( {f_1N_0,\ldots , f_nN_0} \right) \in \Delta ^{\theta }$$\end{document}XDθ(0)=f1N0,…,fnN0∈Δθ for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta $$\end{document}θ∈Θ, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}({D})=\mathcal {J}'({D})$$\end{document}J(D)=J′(D) for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D} \in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}D∈U.

Proof

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}\in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}D∈U and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${X_D^\theta }(0) = \left( {f_1N_0,\ldots , f_nN_0} \right) \in \Delta ^{\theta }$$\end{document}XDθ(0)=f1N0,…,fnN0∈Δθ for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta $$\end{document}θ∈Θ, then Lemma 2.1 implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${X_D^\theta (t)} \in \Delta ^{\theta } {\subset \Delta }$$\end{document}XDθ(t)∈Δθ⊂Δ for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${t}\in [0,T]$$\end{document}t∈[0,T]. Recalling that, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta $$\end{document}θ∈Θ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_0^\theta \equiv \tilde{F}_0^\theta $$\end{document}F0θ≡F~0θ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_1^\theta \equiv \tilde{F}_1^\theta $$\end{document}F1θ≡F~1θ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{2{K_{\max }}}(0)\supset \Delta $$\end{document}B2Kmax(0)⊃Δ (see eq. (2.7)), it turns out that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}({D})=\mathcal {J}'({D})$$\end{document}J(D)=J′(D). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

In view of Lemma 2.3, we now address the computation of the differential of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}'$$\end{document}J′. The fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}'$$\end{document}J′ is defined on the whole \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 simplifies the arguments. Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D} \in L^2([0,T],\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}D∈L2([0,T],R), we compute the differential of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}'$$\end{document}J′, and we represent it as an element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2([0,T],\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}L2([0,T],R) via the Riesz’s isometry. Taking advantage of the results obtained in Scagliotti (2023a), we first consider the mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2([0,T],\mathbb {R}) \ni {D}\mapsto {X_D^\theta }(t) \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}L2([0,T],R)∋D↦XDθ(t)∈Rn when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${t} \in [0,T]$$\end{document}t∈[0,T] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta $$\end{document}θ∈Θ are fixed.

Proposition 2.5

For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta $$\end{document}θ∈Θ and for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in [0,T]$$\end{document}σ∈[0,T], we define2.20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {g_D^\theta }(\sigma ) :=\int _\sigma ^T \ell ' \left( {N_D^\theta }({t}) \right) (1,{\ldots ,}1)\cdot M_{D}^\theta ({t}) \,\textrm{d}{t} \left( M_{D}^{\theta }(\sigma ) \right) ^{-1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}gDθ(σ):=∫σTℓ′NDθ(t)(1,…,1)·MDθ(t)dtMDθ(σ)-1,and, by combining eq. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma =T$$\end{document}σ=T, we directly read from eq.
