Proposition 2.5 yields the following result for the differential of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J related to the model of interest.

Corollary 2.6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}:\mathcal {U}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}J:U→R be defined as in eq. (2.8), and let us consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D,D'}\in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}D,D′∈U and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \in [0,1]$$\end{document}ε∈[0,1]. Then, we have2.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \frac{\mathcal {J}({D+\varepsilon (D'-D)})- \mathcal {J}({D})}{\varepsilon } = \int _0^T \left( \int _\Theta {g_D^\theta }(\sigma ) F_1^\theta \left( {X_D^\theta }(\sigma ) \right) \,\textrm{d}\mu (\theta ) \right) {(D'(\sigma )-D(\sigma ))} \,\textrm{d}\sigma , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limε→0J(D+ε(D′-D))-J(D)ε=∫0T∫ΘgDθ(σ)F1θXDθ(σ)dμ(θ)(D′(σ)-D(σ))dσ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${g_D^\theta } :[0,T]\rightarrow (\mathbb {R}^{n})^*$$\end{document}gDθ:[0,T]→(Rn)∗ solves eq. (2.17) for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta $$\end{document}θ∈Θ.

Proof

Recalling that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}\subset L^2([0,T],\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}U⊂L2([0,T],R) is convex, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D+\varepsilon (D'-D)}\in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}D+ε(D′-D)∈U for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \in [0,1]$$\end{document}ε∈[0,1]. Hence, Lemma 2.3 guarantees that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}({D+\varepsilon (D'-D)})=\mathcal {J}'({D+\varepsilon (D'-D)})$$\end{document}J(D+ε(D′-D))=J′(D+ε(D′-D)) for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \in [0,1]$$\end{document}ε∈[0,1]. Therefore, we conclude using Proposition 2.5. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

From Corollary 2.6 we readily get the representation of the differential of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J. Namely, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}\in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}D∈U, owing to eq. (2.22) we can represent through Riesz’s isometry the differential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _{D}\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}∇DJ as2.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}({t}) :=\int _\Theta {g_D^\theta }({t}) F_1^\theta \left( {X_D^\theta }({t}) \right) \,\textrm{d}\mu (\theta ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇DJ(t):=∫ΘgDθ(t)F1θXDθ(t)dμ(θ)for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${t} \in [0,T]$$\end{document}t∈[0,T]. We conclude this part by observing the structure of a gradient-based algorithm for the minimization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J. We first investigate the tangent cone to the set of admissible control \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U at a point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}\in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}D∈U. In doing this, we follow the notion provided in (Mordukhovich 2006, Definition 1.8), i.e.,2.24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T({D},\mathcal {U}) :=\limsup _{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal {U}- {D}}{\varepsilon }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T(D,U):=lim supε→0U-Dε,where the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\limsup $$\end{document}lim sup in eq. (2.24) is understood as the collection of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-strong limiting points of sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(v_{\varepsilon _n})_n$$\end{document}(vεn)n such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{\varepsilon _n}\in \frac{\mathcal {U}- {D}}{\varepsilon _n}$$\end{document}vεn∈U-Dεn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _n\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}εn→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞.

Lemma 2.7

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}\subset L^2([0,T],\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}U⊂L2([0,T],R) be defined as in eq. (2.6). Then, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}\in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}D∈U, we have that2.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T({D},\mathcal {U}) = \left\{ v \in L^2([0,T],\mathbb {R}) : v({t}) \le 0 \,\,\, \textrm{ if }\,\,\, {D}({t})={D_{\max }},\, v(\tau ) \ge 0 \,\,\, \textrm{ if }\,\,\, {D}({t})=0 \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T(D,U)=v∈L2([0,T],R):v(t)≤0ifD(t)=Dmax,v(τ)≥0ifD(t)=0.

Proof

See Appendix A. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We introduce the mappings \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _\mathcal {U}:L^2([0,T],\mathbb {R}) \rightarrow \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}ΠU:L2([0,T],R)→U and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _{T({D},\mathcal {U})} :L^2([0,T],\mathbb {R}) \rightarrow T({D},\mathcal {U})$$\end{document}ΠT(D,U):L2([0,T],R)→T(D,U) defined as the projections onto the closed convex set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U and onto the closed convex cone \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T({D},\mathcal {U})$$\end{document}T(D,U), respectively. With a direct computation, it is possible to show that2.26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Pi _\mathcal {U}[v]({t}) = \max \big (\min \big ( v({t}), {D_{\max }}\big ), 0\big ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΠU[v](t)=max(min(v(t),Dmax),0)and that2.27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Pi _{T({D},\mathcal {U})}[v]({t}) = \max \big ( v({t}), 0 \big ) 1\!\!1_{\{{D}=0\}} + \min \big ( v({t}), 0 \big ) 1\!\!1_{\{{D}={D_{\max }}\}} + v({t})1\!\!1_{\{0<{D}<{D_{\max }}\}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΠT(D,U)[v](t)=max(v(t),0)1{D=0}+min(v(t),0)1{D=Dmax}+v(t)1{0<D<Dmax}for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${t}\in [0,T]$$\end{document}t∈[0,T], for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}\in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}D∈U and for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in L^2([0,T],\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}v∈L2([0,T],R).

Proposition 2.8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}:\mathcal {U}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}J:U→R be defined as in eq. (2.8), and let us consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta >0$$\end{document}η>0. Then, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}\in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}D∈U we have that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Pi _\mathcal {U}\left[ {D} - \eta \nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}\right] = \Pi _\mathcal {U}\left[ {D} + \eta \Pi _{T({D},\mathcal {U})} [-\nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}] \right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΠUD-η∇DJ=ΠUD+ηΠT(D,U)[-∇DJ].

Proof

To ease the notations, let us define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1 :={D} - \eta \nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}$$\end{document}v1:=D-η∇DJ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_2 :={D} + \eta \Pi _{T({D},\mathcal {U})} [-\nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}]$$\end{document}v2:=D+ηΠT(D,U)[-∇DJ]. From eq. (2.26) and (2.27), we notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}$$\end{document}-∇DJ can differ from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _{T({D},\mathcal {U})} [-\nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}]$$\end{document}ΠT(D,U)[-∇DJ] only on those points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${t}\in [0,T]$$\end{document}t∈[0,T] such that either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}({t})=0$$\end{document}D(t)=0 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}({t})={D_{\max }}$$\end{document}D(t)=Dmax.

Let us assume that for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${t}\in [0,T]$$\end{document}t∈[0,T] we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}({t})=0$$\end{document}D(t)=0. On the one hand, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}({t})>0$$\end{document}-∇DJ(t)>0, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}({t}) = \Pi _{T({D},\mathcal {U})} [-\nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}]$$\end{document}-∇DJ(t)=ΠT(D,U)[-∇DJ], yielding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1({t})=v_2({t})$$\end{document}v1(t)=v2(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _\mathcal {U}[v_1]({t})= \Pi _\mathcal {U}[v_2]({t})$$\end{document}ΠU[v1](t)=ΠU[v2](t). On the other hand, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}({t})<0$$\end{document}-∇DJ(t)<0, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _{T({D},\mathcal {U})} [-\nabla _{D} \mathcal {J}]=0$$\end{document}ΠT(D,U)[-∇DJ]=0, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_2({t})=0$$\end{document}v2(t)=0, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1({t})<0$$\end{document}v1(t)<0. However, using eq. (2.26), we deduce that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _\mathcal {U}[v_1]({t})= 0 = \Pi _\mathcal {U}[v_2]({t})$$\end{document}ΠU[v1](t)=0=ΠU[v2](t).

The argument for t such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}({t})={D_{\max }}$$\end{document}D(t)=Dmax is analogous. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 5

The previous result suggests an implementable approach for the numerical minimization of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J. Given a current guess \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}_n\in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}Dn∈U, we perform the update \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}_{n+1}:=\Pi _{\mathcal {U}}[{D}_n - \eta \nabla _{{D}_n}\mathcal {J}]$$\end{document}Dn+1:=ΠU[Dn-η∇DnJ]. Proposition 2.8 ensures that we do not need to take care of projecting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\nabla _{{D}_n}\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}-∇DnJ onto the tangent cone to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}_n$$\end{document}Dn. The convergence properties of the proposed projected gradient scheme (in particular, convergence to stationary points) follow from classical results on projected gradient methods in Hilbert spaces; see (Bertsekas 1999, Chapter 2).

Ensemble Optimal Control in Action: Numerical Experiments

In this section, we first describe the framework where we set our numerical experiments2, i.e., the androgen deprivation therapy in prostate cancer. Then, we present the benchmark strategies and we discuss the results obtained through the resolution of ensemble optimal control problems. Finally, taking advantage of the insights provided by this viewpoint, we introduce a variant of the adaptive therapy proposed in Gatenby et al. (2009); Zhang et al. (2017).

In the remainder of this paper, we work within the two-population model mentioned in the Introduction (see eq. (1.5) and Table 2). As usual, in order to get a non-dimensional system of equations, we perform in eq. (1.5) the time reparametrization and the function transformations as follows:3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\begin{matrix} & \tau :=r_S t, \quad s(\tau ) :=\frac{S(r_S t)}{K}, \quad r(\tau ) :=\frac{R(r_S t)}{K},\\ & n(\tau ) :=\frac{N(r_S t)}{K}, \quad u(\tau ) :=\frac{D(r_S t)}{D_{\max }}. \end{matrix}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}τ:=rSt,s(τ):=S(rSt)K,r(τ):=R(rSt)K,n(τ):=N(rSt)K,u(τ):=D(rSt)Dmax.We observe that in eq. Non-dimensional variables and parameters of the simplified model.

We insist on the fact that eq. (3.3) does not include a mechanism of secondary (or ‘acquired’) resistance, and the population \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r^\theta (\tau )$$\end{document}rθ(τ) at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau >0$$\end{document}τ>0 is made of descendants of the resistant clones \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_0^\theta $$\end{document}r0θ present from the very beginning. This hypothesis is frequently assumed (see, e.g., Cunningham et al. 2018; Strobl et al. 2021) and is consistent with the bio-medical evidences concerning the heterogeneity of cancer cells (Swanton 2012).

Framework and Parameters Setting

In this part, we consider the parameters appearing in eq. (3.3) and (3.4) and define the ranges where they vary. In doing that, we take advantage of the estimates provided in Strobl et al. (2021) for prostate cancer treated with androgen deprivation therapy (ADT). We recall that the analysis in Strobl et al. (2021) relied on the results of the trial described in Bruchovsky et al. (2006). There, a cohort of patients with biochemical recurrence of the tumor (non-metastatic and castration sensitive, m0CSPC) after radical radiotherapy treatment underwent intermittent ADT.

Parameters not affected by uncertainty

We begin by listing the parameters that in our experiments are assumed to be unaffected by uncertainty. This restriction is motivated by the fact that some parameters are taken from well-established estimates available in the literature. Furthermore, limiting the number of uncertain parameters allows us to study the variability in treatment response, while keeping the ensemble optimal control problem computationally tractable.

The first crucial quantity to be estimated is the proliferation rate for sensitive cells \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_S$$\end{document}rS, which is employed to perform the time-normalization of the original system (1.5) into (3.3). In Strobl et al. (2021), the authors set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_S=0.027\, \textrm{day}^{-1}$$\end{document}rS=0.027day-1, adopted from a previous estimate derived in Zhang et al. (2017). Then, the effectiveness of the therapy is encoded in the value of the non-dimensional constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{d}_D$$\end{document}d^D, which is set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{d}_D=1.5$$\end{document}d^D=1.5 in Strobl et al. (2021) (see also West et al. 2019 for the original estimate). We recall that the normalized dimension of the tumor is expressed using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=n(\tau )$$\end{document}n=n(τ), which ranges in [0, 1]. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=0$$\end{document}n=0 means that the tumor cells (sensitive and resistant) are entirely extinct, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=1$$\end{document}n=1 implies that the tumor size has reached the system’s carrying capacity. The initial size of the tumor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n(0)=n_0$$\end{document}n(0)=n0 in our experiments takes values in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{0.25, 0.50, 0.75\}\ni n_0$$\end{document}{0.25,0.50,0.75}∋n0. We insist that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0$$\end{document}n0 is not affected by uncertainty, and we shall run the simulations separately for the different values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0$$\end{document}n0. Finally, in Strobl et al. (2021) the turnover rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{d}_T$$\end{document}d^T is proposed to range in the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{d}_T\in [0,0.5]$$\end{document}d^T∈[0,0.5]. In the present paper, we consider the most adverse scenario, i.e., we always set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{d}_T=0$$\end{document}d^T=0.

Parameters affected by uncertainty

We now describe the parameters that are affected by uncertainty. The first quantity is the normalized proliferation rate of the resistant tumor cells encoded in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{r}_R$$\end{document}r^R. In Strobl et al. (2021) this constant is suggested to vary in the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0.5,1]\ni \hat{r}_R$$\end{document}[0.5,1]∋r^R. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{r}_R= 0.5$$\end{document}r^R=0.5 means that the ‘cost’ of having the drug resistance results in the proliferation rate being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$50\%$$\end{document}50% smaller than that of sensitive cells. Conversely, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{r}_R= 1$$\end{document}r^R=1, the resistant cells have no disadvantage towards the sensitive ones regarding reproduction rate.

In the ODE model considered here, there is no mechanism of resistance acquirement during the dynamics. In other words, in our framework, the population of resistant cells \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(\tau )$$\end{document}r(τ) at an instant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau >0$$\end{document}τ>0 consists of clones of an initial resistant subpopulation that is assumed to be present from the very beginning. This hypothesis is consistent with the heterogeneity and polyclonal nature of solid cancers, which in the clinical practice is particularly realistic in the case of advanced or metastatic disease (see e.g. Swanton 2012). We assume that the initial fraction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{f}_0$$\end{document}f^0 of resistant cells ranges in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0.002,0.1]\ni \hat{f}_0$$\end{document}[0.002,0.1]∋f^0, meaning that the ratio \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{f}_0:=r(0)/(r(0)+s(0))$$\end{document}f^0:=r(0)/(r(0)+s(0)) varies between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.2\%$$\end{document}0.2% (most favourable scenario) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10\%$$\end{document}10% (worst-case scenario). In future work, we plan to incorporate a feature that accounts for the secondary resistance mechanism into the model.

Space of parameters and ensemble measure

We are in a position to provide a formal definition for the space of parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta $$\end{document}Θ where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta :=(\hat{d}_D, \hat{d}_T, \hat{r}_R, \hat{f}_0)$$\end{document}θ:=(d^D,d^T,r^R,f^0) takes values. According to what was said above, in our experiments we consider\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta = \{1.5\} \times \{0\} \times [0.5,1] \times [0.002,0.1]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θ={1.5}×{0}×[0.5,1]×[0.002,0.1].In the current preliminary work, we assume the uncertain parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{r}_R, \hat{f}_0$$\end{document}r^R,f^0 to be independent and uniformly distributed in the respective intervals where they range. This hypothesis implies that the probability measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in \mathcal {P}(\Theta )$$\end{document}μ∈P(Θ) used for defining the ensemble optimal control problem has the form:3.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu :=\delta _{1.5} \otimes \delta _0 \otimes U(0.5,1) \otimes U(0.002,0.1), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ:=δ1.5⊗δ0⊗U(0.5,1)⊗U(0.002,0.1),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _{z}$$\end{document}δz denotes the Dirac delta centered at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}z∈R, and U(a, b) denotes the uniform probability distribution over the interval [a, b].

Remark 6

The theoretical machinery developed in Section 2 is completely general and does not require the independence of the different parameters that appear in the model. If, in the future, evidence of correlations between such parameters is observed, it will suffice to adapt accordingly the choice of the measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ. Similarly, if we wished to consider random fluctuations in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{d}_D$$\end{document}d^D or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{d}_T$$\end{document}d^T, we could right away include these features in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta $$\end{document}Θ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ.

As observed in Remark 3, any ensemble optimal control problem related to the measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ defined as in eq. (3.5) requires the simultaneous solution of infinitely many ODEs, just for evaluating the objective functional. Since this is unfeasible for simulations, we pursue the approach provided by Theorem 2.2. Namely, we approximate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ with a discrete probability measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _k$$\end{document}μk with finite support, and we address the minimization of the functional related to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _k$$\end{document}μk. We emphasize that the construction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _k$$\end{document}μk detailed below should be understood as a proof of concept. In a more realistic scenario, one can consider repeated measurements of tumor burden in a cohort of k patients and estimate the model parameters using techniques from system identification (see, e.g., Nelles 2020). The model parameters for each patient, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _1,\ldots ,\theta _k$$\end{document}θ1,…,θk, are then interpreted as independent samples from the measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ, and the resulting empirical probability measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mu _k :=\frac{1}{k} \sum _{i=1}^k \delta _{\theta _i}$$\end{document}μk:=1k∑i=1kδθi is used in the definition of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}_k$$\end{document}Jk. A detailed description of such parameter estimation procedures is beyond the scope of the present manuscript and is left for future development. Our focus here is on the mathematical formulation and analysis of the ensemble optimal control problem, rather than on the practical implementation of parameter estimation.

In our specific setting, we discretized the interval [0.5, 1] where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{r}_R$$\end{document}r^R varies into 25 equispaced nodes with distance 0.02. Hence, we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(0.5,1)\approx \frac{1}{25}\sum _{j=1}^{25} \delta _{\hat{r}_R^j}$$\end{document}U(0.5,1)≈125∑j=125δr^Rj, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{r}_R^j\in \{0.5, 0.52,\ldots ,1\}$$\end{document}r^Rj∈{0.5,0.52,…,1}. Regarding the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{f}_0$$\end{document}f^0, we divided the interval [0.002, 1] into 49 nodes with a constant step equal to 0.002. Then, we considered \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(0.002,0.1)\approx \frac{1}{49}\sum _{k=1}^{49} \delta _{\hat{f}_0^k}$$\end{document}U(0.002,0.1)≈149∑k=149δf^0k, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{f}_0^k\in \{0.002, 0.004,\ldots ,0.1\}$$\end{document}f^0k∈{0.002,0.004,…,0.1}. Finally, we defined3.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta _N :=\{1.5\} \times \{0\} \times \{0.5,0.52,\ldots ,1\} \times \{0.002,0.004, \ldots , 0.1\}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΘN:={1.5}×{0}×{0.5,0.52,…,1}×{0.002,0.004,…,0.1},and3.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _N:=\delta _{1.5} \otimes \delta _0 \otimes \frac{1}{25}\sum _{j=1}^{25} \delta _{\hat{r}_R^j} \otimes \frac{1}{49}\sum _{k=1}^{49} \delta _{\hat{f}_0^k}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μN:=δ1.5⊗δ0⊗125∑j=125δr^Rj⊗149∑k=149δf^0k,which has a support consisting of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N=1225$$\end{document}N=1225 points. From a practical perspective, the introduction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _N$$\end{document}μN results in performing the numerical experiments on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N=1225$$\end{document}N=1225 different tumors, corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^{j,k}=(1.5,0,\hat{r}_R^j, \hat{f}_0^k)$$\end{document}θj,k=(1.5,0,r^Rj,f^0k) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=1,\ldots ,25$$\end{document}j=1,…,25 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1,\ldots ,49$$\end{document}k=1,…,49. We used these simulated tumors to evaluate every therapy schedule that we considered.

Numerical integration scheme

For every treatment schedule that we tested, we approximated the dynamics of the control systems (3.3) using the Explicit Euler method with stepsize \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _{\textrm{discr}}=r_S/8=3.375\cdot 10^{-3}$$\end{document}τdiscr=rS/8=3.375·10-3. In the non-rescaled system (1.5), this choice corresponds to dividing each simulation day into 8 equispaced time nodes.

Time-To-Progression (TTP)

In this paper, we measure the performances of the different strategies of drug scheduling using the Time-To-Progression (TTP), i.e., the length of the period from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau =0$$\end{document}τ=0 to the first instant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _{\textrm{TTP}}>0$$\end{document}τTTP>0 when the tumor is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$20\%$$\end{document}20% bigger (in terms of population) than the initial size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0$$\end{document}n0. The disease is said ‘to be in progression’ when this condition occurs. More precisely, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta _N$$\end{document}θ∈ΘN we set3.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau _{\textrm{TTP}}^\theta :=\inf _{\tau >0}\{ s^\theta (\tau ) + r^\theta (\tau ) \ge 1.2\, n_0 \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}τTTPθ:=infτ>0{sθ(τ)+rθ(τ)≥1.2n0}.We report that this quantity was employed also in Strobl et al. (2021), and we recall that is related to the radiologically-defined criterion ‘RECIST’ (see Eisenhauer et al. 2009). In view of the strategies resulting from ensemble optimal control problems, it is convenient to introduce a variant of TTP, which we denote with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {TTP}^\prime $$\end{document}TTP′. We define it as follows:3.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau _{\mathrm {TTP'}}^\theta :=\sup _{\tau >0}\{ s^\theta (\tau ) + r^\theta (\tau ) \le 1.2\, n_0 \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}τTTP′θ:=supτ>0{sθ(τ)+rθ(τ)≤1.2n0}for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta _N$$\end{document}θ∈ΘN.

Remark 7

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s^\theta (0) + r^\theta (0)=n_0< 1.2 \, n_0$$\end{document}sθ(0)+rθ(0)=n0<1.2n0, the continuity of the mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \mapsto s^\theta (\tau ) + r^\theta (\tau )$$\end{document}τ↦sθ(τ)+rθ(τ) for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _{\textrm{TTP}}^\theta \le \tau _{\mathrm {TTP'}}^\theta $$\end{document}τTTPθ≤τTTP′θ. If for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ there exists a unique instant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\tau }>0$$\end{document}τ¯>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s^\theta (\bar{\tau }) + r^\theta (\bar{\tau }) = 1.2\, n_0$$\end{document}sθ(τ¯)+rθ(τ¯)=1.2n0, then it turns out that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _{\textrm{TTP}}^\theta = \tau _{\mathrm {TTP'}}^\theta = \bar{\tau }$$\end{document}τTTPθ=τTTP′θ=τ¯.

Benchmark Approaches: MTD and Adaptive Therapy

We begin by describing the benchmark therapies for evaluating the performances of the schedules proposed later. The baseline is the Maximal Tolerated Dose protocol (MTD), which consists of constantly giving the patient the maximal quantity of the drug. In our model, this results in setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\textrm{MTD}}(\tau ) = 1$$\end{document}uMTD(τ)=1 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau >0$$\end{document}τ>0. When the competition between sensitive and resistant clones for shared resources is relevant (like in the present model), MTD has already been shown to be sub-optimal. For instance, the role played by this Darwinian mechanism was highlighted in Silva et al. (2012). Namely, the MTD leads rapidly to the extinction of the sensitive population, resulting in a quick and dramatic shrinking of the tumor size. After observing partial or total regression of the disease for a certain amount of time—whose duration depends indeed on the proliferation rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{r}_R$$\end{document}r^R of the resistant cells—, a resurgence of the tumor is typically observed, and the resistant cells are predominant.

Adaptive Therapy (AT) has been introduced in Gatenby et al. (2009) for enhancing the long-time management of the disease, and it has been proven effective in several studies involving both numerical simulations (Zhang et al. 2017) and a pilot study with patients (Zhang et al. 2017, 2022). The key idea is to adaptively introduce vacation periods when drug treatment is discontinued. Here, we apply the strategy as described in Strobl et al. (2021) on the ensemble of tumors modeled by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _N$$\end{document}ΘN (see eq. (3.6)) and which we simulate the dynamics of. More precisely, the AT relies on the following steps, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \Theta _N$$\end{document}θ∈ΘN: At \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau =0$$\end{document}τ=0, set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=1$$\end{document}u=1 and keep it constant while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s^\theta (\tau ) + r^\theta (\tau )> n_0/2$$\end{document}sθ(τ)+rθ(τ)>n0/2 (treatment period).After observing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s^\theta (\tau )+r^\theta (\tau )\le n_0/2$$\end{document}sθ(τ)+rθ(τ)≤n0/2, reset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=0$$\end{document}u=0 and keep it constant while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s^\theta (\tau ) + r^\theta (\tau )< n_0$$\end{document}sθ(τ)+rθ(τ)<n0 (vacation period).After observing again \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s^\theta (\tau ) + r^\theta (\tau ) \ge n_0$$\end{document}sθ(τ)+rθ(τ)≥n0, reset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=1$$\end{document}u=1 and go to Step (1).To distinguish between this approach and the variant of AT that we will discuss later, we refer to the strategy just described as ‘On-Off’ AT. The results are reported in Table 4. The values of the TTP in Table 4 are perfectly consistent with the findings shown in (Strobl et al. (2021), Figure 3.A). Namely, on the one hand, the ‘On-Off’ AT never performed worse than the classical MTD. On the other hand, the advantage of the introduction of ‘treatment vacation periods’ in AT is apparent when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0=0.5$$\end{document}n0=0.5 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0=0.75$$\end{document}n0=0.75, where the progression of the disease is delayed respectively of 2 and 7 weeks on average, if compared to the mean TTP achieved using MTD. In fig. 1, we plotted the dynamics of the tumor populations (sensitive, resistant, and total) when treated with MTD and ‘On-Off’ AT, for a specific value of the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta = (\hat{d}_D, \hat{d}_T, \hat{r}_R, \hat{f}_0)$$\end{document}θ=(d^D,d^T,r^R,f^0). For such a value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ, ‘On-Off’ AT dramatically outperforms MTD. On the one hand, in MTD the sensitive subpopulation is rapidly extinct, and it is replaced by the resistant cells, which do not have any biological competitor for resources. On the other hand, in ‘On-Off’ AT the ‘treatment vacation periods’ allow for restocking the sensitive subpopulation, preventing its extinction. This results in an increased fight between sensitive and resistant cells for shared resources, contributing to the delay of the progression of the disease. Moreover, we notice that in ‘On-Off’ AT we can directly read the treatment-vacation cycles from the graph of the populations’ dynamics.Fig. 1Dynamics of the tumor corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta = (1.5,0,0.66,0.01)$$\end{document}θ=(1.5,0,0.66,0.01) and with initial size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0=0.5$$\end{document}n0=0.5 undergoing MTD (left) and ‘On-Off’ AT (right). The dashed horizontal line represents the threshold tumor size related to the condition ‘cancer in progression’. For this \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ, the advantage of ‘On-Off’ AT (right picture) over MTD (left picture) is apparent. The TTP of ‘On-Off’ AT and MTD is 424 days and 370 days, respectively, with a progression delay of almost 8 weeks (color figure online)

Dynamics of the tumor corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta = (1.5,0,0.66,0.01)$$\end{document}θ=(1.5,0,0.66,0.01) and with initial size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0=0.5$$\end{document}n0=0.5 undergoing MTD (left) and ‘On-Off’ AT (right). Ensemble Optimal Control Problems: Formulation and Resolution

In this part, we consider the problem of finding a therapy schedule \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=u(\tau )$$\end{document}u=u(τ) for the control system (3.3), in the case some of the parameters appearing in (3.3) are affected by uncertainty. We insist that, when solving an ensemble optimal control problem, we aim to find a shared policy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=u(\tau )$$\end{document}u=u(τ) that shall be used for every admissible tuple of parameters. In Subsection 3.1 we introduced the space of parameters and the discrete measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _N$$\end{document}μN. Before addressing the resolution of the ensemble optimal control problem, we are left to set the time horizon for the controlled dynamics and the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell :\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ℓ:R→R that designs the integral cost.

Simulation interval For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0\in \{0.25,0.50,0.75\}$$\end{document}n0∈{0.25,0.50,0.75} we set the value of the time horizon \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\textrm{hor}}$$\end{document}Thor according to the maximal value of TTP observed in Table 4. We collect the selected values in Table 5, where we also reported the normalized time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T:=T_{\textrm{hor}} \cdot r_S$$\end{document}T:=Thor·rS that is required in the definition of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J (see eq. (2.8)).
